### Page 11

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 4   |   P R ES S U R E ON EU R OP E
Europe will be expected to show how ambition can be delivered given calls for greater ambition
11
GDP, Emissions and UNFCCC Classification by Country
(Wealthier Non-Annex I countries are labelled, as is the USA)
Source: World Bank, EDGAR, Deutsche Bank Research
Exiting COP31, there will be more pressure on the European nations to back up their stance that the COP31 final draft text (i.e., headline agreement) did not go far enough to address

climate change. The EU, UK, Norway and Switzerland all either formally rejected or voiced opposition to the draft text. Countries choosing to walk away will be expected to show how
they could deliver the ambition they say the text lacked, including finance for developing countries. With public finance constrained (a point of contention and driver of constraint at
this year’s COP), this means enabling private capital and driving the regulation needed to mobilise it.

The collapse of COP31 cover text negotiations should not be viewed as a shock in
isolation; arguably, this outcome has been building up over recent COPs.
Last year at COP30, EU negotiators reportedly considered walking away from talks over
dissatisfaction regarding the lack of fossil fuel language, yet they ultimately decided to accept
the deal after receiving limited concessions, with a broader sense among EU figures at the
time that it was better to keep the process moving forwards as opposed to collapsing it

entirely. However, post-COP30 discussions among EU members reportedly included a push
from some for clearer guidance on when to walk away, meaning COP31’s outcome was seen
as possible.
Having rejected the final cover text on the grounds that it was not ambitious enough,
Europe will face pressure to show how to deliver these stronger outcomes
By taking a hard line on including fossil fuel transition language in the text, Europe will be
expected to support the development of incentives and frameworks that enable more

ambitious outcomes, particularly with respect to incentivising private capital. The role of
multilateral development banks will likely be a focus, while guarantees and risk-sharing are
viewed as some of the most effective methods of scaling up private finance involvement.
Reports that China would bid to host COP33 proved false
China remains the world’s largest emitter, accounting for around a third of global emissions,
yet it has made considerable progress, often highlighted in detail at its COP pavilion. Its CO2

emissions have been broadly flat or falling since early 2024, driven by record growth in wind
and solar. China clearly wants the net zero transition to move forward, given its significant
investment in climate technology, on which the rest of the world, including Europe and the
Global South, is increasingly reliant. Notably, however, it decided not to bid to host COP33 in
2028, despite hopes from some that such an event would help fill the leadership gap left by

the US.

TAK EAW AY 5   |   S CI ENCE & THE I P CC
No agreement on the timeline for the IPCC reports key to informing the 2028 stocktake

12
A recurring concern among attendees was a potential delay to the IPCC’s next assessment reports. The concern was twofold. First, the reports are needed to inform the second global

stocktake in 2028. Second, our discussions suggest there is concern that climate deterioration is happening faster than initially expected, thus delay risks decisions resting on out-of-
date science. Even if there is agreement to produce the reports in time, there is concern that it can’t be fully accurate, as the timeline may limit contributions from scientists in
developing countries, who are struggling to secure the funding and capacity needed to contribute within the shorter timeframe.

IPCC report timelines were a point of contention at COP31 discussions
The Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change (IPCC) is the UN body for assessing the scientific
evidence on climate change, and its assessment reports provide the scientific basis for climate
negotiations. The IPCC produces reports in 3 areas, namely 1) Physical Science, 2) Impacts,
Adaptation and Vulnerability, and 3) Mitigation, with the last such reports published prior to the

conclusion of the first global stocktake (which measures progress towards goals set out in the
Paris Agreement and informs the next round of national climate plans, known as NDCs) at COP28
in 2023. Coming into COP31, countries were aiming to agree on the timing for these reports
(having failed to reach agreement at previous IPCC meetings), such that they could be signed off at
the IPCC’s next meeting in December.
Europe pushed for faster IPCC reports, while other nations favoured delay

Typically, the IPCC reports have operated on a 5–7 year cycle, but the exact timings require
agreement among IPCC members. At COP31, European diplomats pushed for a trajectory that
would allow the next reports to inform the second global stocktake, which opened at COP31 and is
due to conclude at COP33 in 2028. Some nations favoured a longer timeline, with Kenya arguing
that scientists from developing countries needed more time to contribute, while India and Saudi

Arabia were also said to favour a delay. Some stakeholders at COP31 suggested the reports will
likely paint an alarming picture of the climate, and publishing them before the stocktake would
add pressure for stronger action.
Discussions will reconvene at December’s IPCC meetings
There is concern that agreement will not be reached in time. The longer the impasse continues,
the less time scientists have to prepare for publication. The IPCC Chair has previously been quoted

as stating that agreement must be reached by the end of 2026 if the reports are to be ready to
contribute to the second stocktake. Alongside (and somewhat linked to) disagreement over the
report timelines, nations will need to reach agreement on the IPCC’s budget for the remainder of
the current cycle.
IPCC reports and the global stocktake: key dates
Dec 2023 | COP28, Dubai
First global stocktake concludes; outcomes used to inform next round of
national climate plans

Nov 2026 | COP31, Antalya
Second global stocktake opens; countries again fail to agree a timeline for
the next IPCC reports
Dec 2026 | IPCC meeting
Discussions to reconvene; the IPCC Chair has said agreement is needed by
end-2026 for the reports to be ready in time to contribute to the 2nd
stocktake
Nov 2028 | COP33 (host unresolved)
Second global stocktake scheduled to conclude; outcome will inform the
next round of global climate plans.
Dec 2015 | COP21, Paris

Countries adopt the Paris Agreement. Article 14 establishes global
stocktakes every five years, unless parties agree otherwise.
Nov 2021 | COP26, Glasgow
First global stocktake begins, with information collection and preparation.
Apr 2022 | IPCC AR6 mitigation report released
IPCC publishes the 3rd AR6 working-group report, on mitigation of climate
change. This gave time for the findings of the 3 reports to contribute to the
first stocktake’s outcomes.

TAK EAW AY 6   |   TR AD E
Trade is still a point of contention

13
Cumulative value of EU imports from CBAM sectors by market (2015-24)
Source: BNEF, Deutsche Bank Research.
Note: Chart displays nominal value of cumulative imports to the EU by source markets.
Developing nations, including China, India and the African Group, pushed back against unilateral climate-related trade measures, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism
(CBAM) a particular target. We expect trade to remain a source of friction in climate diplomacy, while cleaner producers should gain a global trade advantage.

Several nations have sought to bring trade into climate discussions at COP
The African Group negotiating bloc submitted a request prior to the event to have an item on
climate-related trade measures formally added to the COP31 agenda. The attempt was not
successful. Meanwhile, both India and China came into COP31 promising to use the event to push
back against climate-related trade mechanisms, with the EU’s Carbon Border Adjustment
Mechanism (CBAM) a particular point of contention. Developed countries continue to defend such

measures, arguing that they are necessary to level the playing field for domestic firms in cases
where foreign competitors face lower environment-related compliance costs relative to domestic
industries.
At COP30, countries agreed to create a separate space away from COP to discuss climate-
related trade measures, yet this did not stop trade entering discussions
A senior member of China’s delegation described the new platform as “not enough”, calling for

stronger cooperation on measures including defining product carbon footprints and traceability of
agricultural goods to prevent deforestation. They also added that EU-China climate relations had
been damaged by trade tensions. Saudi Arabia and Russia also raised trade during COP31
discussions. A call in the first draft of the cover text “to phase out unilateral climate-related and
trade-restrictive measures” by the end of 2027 had no prospect of success, yet it reflects the desire

of developing countries to continue raising this topic at COP.
In some negotiating tracks, disputes over trade contributed to the collapse of discussions
In a sign of how prevalent trade was in Antalya, an EU official claimed that there was “no room” at
the talks where trade was not raised, describing this as “not helpful”. Indeed, trade was also cited
as the factor behind the collapse of COP31 negotiations on climate change and agriculture, after

some parties demanded that the talks discuss the EU’s CBAM due to claims over its impacts on
fertilisers.

### Page 14

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
There was notable support for electrification goals
The COP Presidency positioned a new “35-by-35” electrification goal as a headline item
for COP31. The goal seeks to raise the share of electricity in global final energy use to
35% by 2035 (vs. c.23% currently) and was generally well received in Antalya, with more
than 80 countries reportedly endorsing it, including China and the EU.
No clean energy requirement drew scrutiny, but was a pragmatic step

As we highlighted in our COP31 preview, the lack of reference to clean energy drew
scrutiny, with concerns that the pledge would fail to deliver climate benefits if fossil fuels
were used to generate the additional electricity. Despite the EU’s eventual endorsement
of “35-by-35”, some member states were reportedly reluctant initially to sign up for this
reason. While we acknowledge these concerns, the pledge could also be viewed as a
well-crafted initiative that addresses energy security while building the infrastructure

needed for significant emissions reductions, even if not immediately. The absence of a
clean energy requirement (though it was implied) was also seen by some as a pragmatic
step that enabled wider participation, as reflected in the high number of nations
pledging support at COP31.
Despite the lack of a clean energy requirement, some countries still opposed the
inclusion of the “35-by-35” goal in the final cover decision
Some developing nations were against its inclusion on the grounds that they viewed it as

potentially restricting development choices. There was also opposition from some of
these countries against the imposition of top-down sectoral targets which do not make
allowances for varying national circumstances. Moreover, while the emphasis of the COP
Presidency on the initiative was likely a key factor behind why it received strong support,
some nations felt that this focus took away from other priorities at the event.

goal)
TAK EAW AY 7   |   EL ECTR I F I CATI ON
Electrification was the clearest common ground, with 80+ countries backing the “35-by-35” goal
14
Electrification was the clearest area of common ground in Antalya. The voluntary “35-by-35” pledge, which aims to raise electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035, was
endorsed by more than 80 countries, including China and the EU, and drew strong business support. The pledge does not formally require clean energy, which drew criticism.

However, we viewed the ambiguity as a way to facilitate broader agreement and grow the infrastructure needed for a more meaningful shift away from fossil fuels. The effort also
aligned with broader energy security goals. We expect continued focus on this topic at future COP events.
Business engagement on electrification at COP31 was high
This was evident in the COP31 Electrification Delivery Package, which sought to highlight
billions in investment to support areas including grids and the electrification of key

sectors such as transport and industry. The UN highlighted that more than 260
businesses with more than $4tn in annual revenue “announced or profiled” actions in
support of these objectives at COP31. Meanwhile, the Utilities for Net Zero Alliance
(UNEZA) announced combined 2026 annual investment plans across grids, renewables
and storage of at least $156bn, a 5% increase on 2025 and a 35% rise vs. 2024.

(2025 vs. 2035 IEA scenarios and the COP31 35-by-35 goal)

### Page 15

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
AI was a missed opportunity, with just 2% of Blue Zone events focused on it
15
AI was a missed opportunity. While the power demands of data centres were acknowledged as a driver of increased energy demand, discussion of the role AI could play in advancing
climate goals was surprisingly limited. Beyond the formal negotiations, COP is also a major educational and climate technology forum, and we had expected the potential for AI to help

accelerate progress towards net zero to feature far more prominently. The Presidency’s Antalya AI Pledge was a welcome step, but drew support from only seven governments, while
support from major AI companies was also limited.
AI-related discussions weren’t as prevalent as we expected
It is unclear whether this reflects fundamental uncertainty among companies about how to scale
AI sustainably or a lack of understanding of how powerful AI could be in solving climate challenges.

In our view, it is likely a combination of both, making this a missed opportunity for COP to educate
companies and governments. That is not to say there weren’t some great AI panels. At one AI
innovation panel we attended, McKinsey noted that AI sales forecasting can help retailers match
ordering to demand and cut avoidable food waste, reducing emissions (roughly a third of food is
never eaten) and delivering monetary savings.
Only 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events featured a notable focus on AI

As a check against our COP experience, we analysed the focus of more than 4,000 panels and
events listed as being held in the Blue Zone at COP31 (those we could gather from the COP31
programme available on the official website). Our AI driven analysis assessed the focus of each
session and determined a maximum of 3 focus topics. Our analysis showed that AI had the lowest
focus of any of the listed topics, with just 2% of COP31 Blue Zone events having a notable focus on

AI. As expected, climate finance and investment was the most frequently addressed topic.
The COP31 Presidency did put AI on the Action Agenda
Türkiye launched the Antalya AI Pledge on 16 November, recognising AI both as a tool for climate
action and a growing source of environmental pressure. Developed with the ITU, this voluntary
framework asks signatories to select measurable commitments, such as powering AI infrastructure
with low- and zero-emission energy and reporting AI’s environmental footprint. By 19 November,

only seven governments had endorsed it, with little support from major AI players, Alibaba being
the notable exception. This could reflect concern that AI will challenge firms’ near-term emission
commitments, especially for tech companies. Here we would highlight a conversation we had with
a leading Chinese solar panel company. When asked how China would handle sustainability issues
from the expected rapid build-out of data centres, an attendee we spoke to said matter of factly:

“China will make data centres sustainable”.

### Page 16

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 9   |   U NI TED  S TATES
The US federal absence weighs on COP, but the midterms boost state-level momentum
16
The absence of the US federal government under the second Trump administration has had a notable negative impact on negotiations and signaling around financing. The Democrats
continue to show up at COP, this year with a message of optimism following the midterm election results. The America Is All In coalition held several panels at COP highlighting climate

commitments and objectives. Democratic governors showed up to remind COP attendees that roughly three-quarters of the US population now have Democratic governors,
strengthening the case that state-level policy will be a key driver of US clean energy and industrial investment. While we tend to agree, the magnitude of the benefit isn’t entirely clear.
The US once again had no federal presence at COP
Explaining the decision to stay away for a second straight COP, a spokesperson for the federal

administration said it was focused on expanding “affordable, reliable, secure energy sources”. This
also meant the US had no formal negotiating presence, though the climate pivot under the second
Trump administration potentially emboldened nations opposed to faster transition timelines.
US State representation and companies did come to Antalya
At the America Is All In pavilion we attended the US Climate Alliance panel “Undaunted”, featuring
Governors Michelle Lujan Grisham (New Mexico) and Tim Walz (Minnesota). Key messages:

– States retain sufficient regulatory authority, economic influence and private sector partnerships
to keep advancing clean energy, industrial decarbonisation and climate resilience, with member
states of the alliance having reduced net greenhouse gas emissions by nearly a quarter
between 2005 and 2024 while also growing their economies by 37%.
– Clean electricity was presented as an industrial competitiveness issue, with manufacturers
demanding reliable access to clean power when deciding where to locate production.

– Affordability and employment were recurring themes, with both governors stressing that
climate policy must deliver visible economic benefits.
– Methane was a focus. New Mexico argued it has combined rising oil and gas output with
stringent methane standards and super-emitter monitoring, resulting in lower emissions.
Governor Grisham signalled hopes of encouraging Texas, which has just elected Democrat Gina
Hinojosa as governor, to adopt similar rules.

– Governor Walz of Minnesota criticised companies that publicly support climate action but locate
manufacturing in states with weaker labour and environmental policies.
– Consistent with our wider observations (see takeaway 8), AI was notably absent from the
discussion, with data centres mentioned only in passing.
Control of State Governorships Post-2026 Midterms
Source: US Department of State, Deutsche Bank.

In the US (and abroad), the level of alarm over global warming is inching higher
As part of our monthly Climate & Sustainability Survey, we ask consumers in the UK, US, Germany, France, Spain and Italy for their views on global warming. In Q3 2026, we recorded a notable
rebound in alarm regarding the pace of global warming, particularly in the UK and Europe following a summer of climate extremes. The level of alarm had begun to dip globally post the 2024 US

presidential election in November.

### Page 18

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
TAK EAW AY 1 0   |   NATU R E, CAR BON MAR K ETS  & OCEANS
Continued scientist alarm over nature and ocean health raises the importance of scaling financing mechanisms
18
Nature and the oceans returned to the agenda at COP31, a month after the UN’s biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) found most 2030 targets off track. Scientists warned of record
ocean heat, and with an overshoot of 1.5°C looking ever more likely, the case for scaling up finance is growing stronger. Canada’s pledge of up to $392.5mn to the Tropical Forest

Forever Facility (TFFF), launched at COP30, gave forest finance a lift, while Brazil presented a deforestation roadmap outside the formal talks. In carbon markets, the focus has moved
from writing the Article 6 rules to making them work. Buyers want high-integrity credits, governments want faster payments. In our view, buyer confidence must come first.
Scientists again warned of concerns over the health of oceans and nature
The UN held a dedicated biodiversity summit (CBD COP17) in Armenia last month. It

found that progress towards most of the 2030 targets under the Kunming-Montreal
Biodiversity Framework has been insufficient. Scientists made similar points at COP31.
The WMO’s 2026 “State of the Climate” report, published during the summit, said ocean
heat content in the upper 2,000 metres reached a record in 2025, with preliminary data
pointing to further increases in 2026.
The value of these systems is well understood, but financing to support their

protection remains a challenge
On a global level, the economic case for protecting these assets is clear, with the World
Bank estimating that more than half of global GDP depends on nature and its services,
while studies put the value of the ocean economy at between $3-6tn. Developing
countries state that while they want to protect oceans and nature, this can often be at
odds with national economic development goals. Finance flows for conservation and

protection initiatives remain relatively small compared with projects in areas such as
clean energy, while developing countries cite misalignment between their needs and
financing conditions, with accessibility a further challenge.
Scaling the financial sector’s involvement will be key
Our analysis shows 2026 is already a record year for blue bond issuance, though it
remains a fraction of green bond issuance. The Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance

(ORRAA), which funds coastal resilience, secured new commitments at COP31, with
Canada pledging CAD$14mn over four years and Australia AUD$1mn over two.
Mangrove Breakthrough, which backs mangrove protection (coastal forests that shield
shorelines, store carbon and support fisheries), said its tracked large-scale projects
reached almost US$1bn in cumulative value since 2020, up 32% year on year.
On carbon markets, implementation is now the main focus for Article 6

Carbon markets let countries and companies offset emissions by paying for projects that
cut or remove them elsewhere. Article 6 of the Paris Agreement sets UN-backed rules for
such trading, and most technical details were settled at earlier COPs. COP31 left the
rulebook intact, with a review due in 2028, so attention turned to implementation. On 16
November the UN climate secretariat soft-launched the first version of an international
registry for Article 6.2, the country-to-country track (Article 6.4 is the centralised UN

mechanism). This international version lets countries without their own registry record
and track credit transfers.
Several Article 6 deals were announced
Norway agreed to buy up to 12 million tonnes of emissions reductions from solar
projects run by Indonesia’s state utility PLN through 2038, at a reported US$20–40 a
tonne. It pays only once reductions are verified and delivered, funding projects that
would otherwise not be viable. Separately, Singapore approved its first Article 6.2 project,

in Rwanda, which will distribute 300,000 ceramic water filters so households can purify
water without boiling it over firewood or charcoal. It is expected to avoid about 780,000
tonnes of CO₂e in 2026–30.
For carbon markets, buyer confidence should remain the priority
Fourteen governments signed the Antalya Statement on Mobilising Private Finance
for Climate Action on 12 November, with a playbook of eight policy levers to grow
corporate demand for high-integrity credits. Corporates and investors still stress the

need for robust credits that deliver the promised emissions reductions and limit
greenwashing risk, while some governments want faster payments and less bureaucracy.
Campaigners warn that lobbying to loosen the rules will intensify before the 2028 review.
In our view, buyer confidence in credit trading processes is paramount and should
remain the priority.
Note: Deutsche Bank is a member of the Ocean Risk and Resilience Action Alliance (ORRAA).

### Page 19

Debbie Jones  |  COP31 Takeaways  |  November 2026
F U TU R E O F  C O P
Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, though questions over how COP will evolve are in focus
COP has shifted from setting targets to implementation. Ethiopia will host COP32 in 2027, where we expect a focus on delivering commitments and climate finance access, reflecting African
priorities. COP33 falls to Asia-Pacific in 2028, though the host remains unconfirmed at present. COP32 and COP33 will test how well multilateralism can respond to the setback at COP31. With a

second Global Stocktake concluding in 2028, its outcome should inform countries’ next national climate plans (NDCs, their emissions-reduction pledges), due in 2030.

Appendix A: Key COP terms used in this report
This appendix explains the main COP process terms and negotiating concepts used throughout the report. Definitions are summarised for non-specialist readers and reflect how each term is
used in this deck.
20
TERM DEFINITION
35-by-35 pledge Türkiye’s voluntary goal to lift electricity’s share of global final energy use to 35% by 2035; endorsed by 80+ countries.

Action Agenda Voluntary, non-binding pledges and initiatives launched alongside the formal talks. It is separate from negotiated decisions.
Annex II / Non-Annex I UNFCCC country classification. Annex II covers developed economies; Non-Annex I covers most other countries, including China and India.
Article 6 Paris Agreement framework including rules on international carbon markets. Article 6.2 covers bilateral trading of credits between countries.

Article 9
Paris Agreement finance provisions. Article 9.1 covers developed
countries’ obligation to provide finance. COP30 set a two-year work
programme for 2026-27.
Baku to Belém Roadmap
to $1.3T
Roadmap to scale climate finance to $1.3tn a year by 2035 from all
sources, inclusive of the $300bn NCQG.
Blue Zone UN-controlled area of the COP venue for formal negotiations, pavilions and side events.
Bonn sessions Mid-year technical talks of the UNFCCC Subsidiary Bodies in Bonn, Germany, which prepare the ground for COP discussions.

Brazil fossil fuel roadmap COP30 Presidency’s voluntary global roadmap for transitioning away from fossil fuels, pursued outside the formal UN negotiations.
CBAM Carbon Border Adjustment Mechanism: EU levy on imports of carbon- intensive goods such as steel, cement, fertiliser and aluminium.
Coalitions of the willing Like-minded countries acting outside the consensus process, such as the 50+ nation Santa Marta process on fossil fuel roadmaps.
Consensus How COP decisions are taken: adopted when no party formally objects, so a single party can theoretically block or dilute an outcome.

COP / COPs Conference of the Parties: the UNFCCC’s annual decision-making summit, where nearly 200 parties negotiate climate action.
COP Presidency The host country’s team that chairs the conference. For COP31, Türkiye’s Murat Kurum was COP President and led the Action Agenda.
Final COP text / cover text Headline decisions adopted by consensus at the close of the COP, setting its formal outcome. Also called the cover decision.
TERM DEFINITION
Formal agenda Negotiated items on the official COP agenda, decided by consensus. Separate from the voluntary Action Agenda.

Global Goal on
Adaptation (GGA)
Paris Agreement goal to strengthen resilience. COP30 adopted 59
indicators; the process to refine these moved forward at COP31.
Global South Informal term for developing and emerging economies, mainly in Africa, Asia and Latin America.
Global Stocktake (GST) Five-yearly review of progress under the Paris Agreement. The second runs from COP31 to COP33 (2028).
IPCC Intergovernmental Panel on Climate Change: the UN body that assesses climate science. Its reports feed the Global Stocktake.

Just transition
mechanism
Belém Action Mechanism agreed at COP30, focused on fairness in the
shift to a low-carbon economy.
Loss and damage fund Fund set up at COP27 and operationalised in 2023 to support vulnerable developing countries.
NCQG New Collective Quantified Goal (COP29): at least $300bn a year for developing countries by 2035, within a $1.3tn ambition.
NDC Nationally determined contribution: each country’s national climate plan, with emissions targets and actions, updated every five years.

Negotiating groups Blocs coordinating national positions: G77 & China, EU, African and Arab Groups, LDCs (least developed) and AOSIS (small island states).
Paris Agreement 2015 treaty to hold warming well below 2°C and pursue 1.5°C, with national climate plans (NDCs) as its core mechanism.
President of Negotiations COP31 role held by Australia’s Chris Bowen, who drafted final texts and steered government-to-government talks.
Temperature overshoot Global warming exceeding the 1.5°C Paris limit, temporarily or permanently, which science now suggests is likely.

UAE Consensus COP28 (Dubai, 2023) agreement to “transition away from fossil fuels”, the first such call in a COP text.
UNFCCC UN Framework Convention on Climate Change: the 1992 treaty behind the climate process. Its parties meet annually at the COP.

### Page 1

After resilient growth this year, several headwinds will likely weigh on the n
economy in 2027. We expect high energy prices to slow consumption over the 
winter, tighter credit conditions to curb demand and France to stagnate until the 
elections. But global manufacturing (supported by AI capex), German fiscal 
expansion and continued structural expansion in Southern Europe should 
cushion these headwinds. We expect Euro area growth of 0.9% in 2027, below 
the recent run rate, estimated potential and consensus.  
High energy prices will likely keep headline inflation near 4% through the winter, n
while indirect effects push core inflation to a 2.9% peak in Q2. But we expect a 
short-lived hump given limited wage second-round effects, with sequential 
inflation pressures largely normalising in 2027H2. 
Above-target inflation and recent ECB commentary point to one more rate hike n
in December. A March move is possible with higher energy prices and stronger 
data, but the hurdle to faster hikes is high. We expect normalisation cuts from 
2027Q4 as the inflation shock fades, leaving our rate path below market pricing. 
Intensifying French budget tensions are the main risk. Polls suggest that Marine n
Le Pen is most likely to become President. If she is elected and wins a majority in 
Parliament, investors could question her commitment to reduce the deficit. If the 
elections fail to deliver a parliamentary majority, the prospects of continued high 
deficits could further weigh on domestic assets. French fiscal tensions could spill 
over into other high-debt countries, but the risk of area-wide contagion looks 
notably lower than in 2011-15. 
Europe also faces structural headwinds from lost competitiveness, rising n
competition from China and demographic changes. Growth industries (such as 
defence), widespread AI adoption and further internal market integration) offer 
significant opportunities, although the upcoming elections will likely hinder 
major reform progress in the near term.

### Page 2

Euro Area 2027 Outlook: Resilience Put to the Test

Following surprisingly resilient Euro area growth this year, we expect 2027 to be more 
challenging. We forecast area-wide growth of 0.9% next year, slightly below the recent 
run rate, our estimate of potential and consensus (Exhibit 1). But we expect growth to 
improve in H2 as headwinds fade and see significant variation across the Euro area, with 
firm growth in Germany and Spain, subdued momentum in Italy and stagnation in 
France.

Rising Headwinds 
We expect several headwinds to slow growth next year, especially in H1. 
First, high energy prices are likely to weigh on consumption over the winter (Exhibit 2). 
Our commodity strategists expect Brent oil and European TTF gas prices to remain 
elevated given continued disruption of Middle East supplies, with risks towards renewed 
escalation and higher prices. As a result, high headline inflation is likely to weigh 
materially on real disposable income over the winter and slow consumer spending. 
Consistent with this, consumer confidence has dropped since the summer and retail 
sales have weakened, especially in Germany.

Exhibit 1: : We Expect Trend-Like Growth in 2027

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Bloomberg
13 November 2026   2
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 3

Second, tighter credit conditions look set to slow growth (Exhibit 3). Following recent 
easing, our area-wide financial condition index (GSFCI) is now only modestly tighter 
since the summer, as a weaker trade-weighted Euro has mitigated the effects of higher 
long-term yields and wider credit spreads. But survey-based measures of bank lending 
have deteriorated notably, especially in France. As a result, credit conditions are likely to 
act as a moderate headwind to growth next year, particularly on rate-sensitive domestic 
activity (such as housing and business investment).

Third, we expect the French economy to stagnate until the elections (Exhibit 4). In 
addition to tightening financial conditions, heightened political and policy uncertainty 
are likely to lead households and firms to postpone major consumption and investment 
decisions. Fiscal policy is likely to remain a contractionary force, because a large 
increase in interest payments will likely mask a modest consolidation in the primary 
balance. We forecast annual average growth of 0.1% in 2027, well below other

Exhibit 3: Tighter Credit Conditions Will Weigh on Growth

13 November 2026   3
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 4

forecasters and the weakest forecast among the developed economies we cover.

Sources of Resilience 
That said, we see several sources of resilience, which should buffer these headwinds and 
help growth recover in H2. 
First, we expect a continued boost from the global manufacturing cycle (Exhibit 5). The 
international industrial upswing continues, boosting factory orders and exports, 
especially in Germany. AI capex is an important driver behind this pickup, likely lifting 
Euro area growth by 0.1pp this year and next. Given spare capacity in European 
manufacturing—particularly in Germany—we see a continued cyclical export tailwind 
ahead.

Second, fiscal policy will continue to support growth in Germany (Exhibit 6). We remain 
constructive on the German fiscal rollout driven by higher defence spending and

Exhibit 4: We Expect Stagnation in France as Fiscal Uncertainty Builds

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Exhibit 5: A Continued Boost from Global Manufacturing, Including AI Capex

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

### Page 5

improved execution of infrastructure investment. We expect the German deficit to rise 
to 4.6% next year (from 4% in 2026) and boost German growth by 0.6pp (vs 0.7pp this 
year). That said, fiscal policy looks set to turn contractionary in France and Spain as 
national consolidation pressures build and the Recovery Fund expires. As a result, we see 
a neutral area-wide fiscal stance in 2027 after a small net boost this year, before turning 
contractionary in 2028.

Third, we see continued structural expansion in Southern Europe (Exhibit 7). Spain, in 
particular, has continued to outgrow the Euro area materially, driven by strong gains in 
employment (underpinned by immigration), higher-value-added services (including 
finance and ICT) and investment. While momentum is likely to moderate next year, we 
see continued outperformance in the South.

### Page 6

Modest Underlying Inflation Pressures 
High energy prices (including product spreads) are likely to keep headline inflation close 
to 4% through the winter (Exhibit 8). Headline inflation could climb even higher if upside 
risks to our commodity price forecasts materialise, with headline inflation peaking close 
to 6% in an adverse scenario for oil and gas. We also see building pressures on food 
inflation next year, reflecting passthrough effects from higher energy prices, the ongoing 
Russia/Ukraine war and global weather patterns.

We do not, however, look for material passthrough effects from headline to underlying 
inflation (Exhibit 9). So far, we see little evidence of indirect effects of higher energy 
prices into core inflation, with muted movement in energy-intensive core goods prices. 
Instead, we estimate that most of the recent pickup in core goods inflation reflects 
AI-related equipment. Given pipeline pressures in import and producer prices as well as 
airfares, we expect more notable indirect effects to build in coming months, pushing 
core inflation up.

Exhibit 8: A Sharp Pickup in Headline Inflation

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   6
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 7

But we expect the core inflation hump to be short-lived given signs of limited 
second-round effects (Exhibit 10). Measures of wage growth have remained contained 
despite the surge in energy prices, including forward-looking surveys (such as the Indeed 
and ECB wage trackers) and recent wage agreements. While we have slightly raised our 
forecast for nominal wage growth next year, we expect pay gains to return to their 
normal range in 2028, driven by moderating labour demand.

Taken together, we expect core inflation to peak at 2.9% in Q2, significantly above the 
ECB’s target and somewhat above the recent staff projections (Exhibit 11), partly 
reflecting firmer energy price assumptions. But we expect the overshoot to be notably 
more limited and temporary than in 2022-23, with sequential inflation pressures largely 
normalising in 2027H2.

Source: Bloomberg, Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research

Exhibit 10: Little Risk of Second-Round Effects

### Page 8

Limited ECB Tightening 
Above-target inflation, ongoing growth and signs that the neutral rate might have edged 
up point to additional ECB tightening, consistent with recent ECB communication and 
our macro model simulations (Exhibit 12). We therefore view one more hike at the 
December meeting as likely. Acute sovereign stress could still lead the Council to hold 
rates in December, but the required tightening in financial conditions would likely need 
to be large given the ongoing energy pressures.

An additional hike in March is possible if energy prices continue to rise, core inflation 
firms more than expected, or clearer signs of second-round effects emerge. But we see a 
significant hurdle to speeding up the pace of hikes and raising the deposit rate 
meaningfully above 3%. We expect the Governing Council to start normalising the policy 
rate as the inflation shock fades and sequential core inflation normalises, with cuts in

Exhibit 11: A Limited Inflation Overshoot

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Exhibit 12: A December Hike is Very Likely

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics
13 November 2026   8
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 9

December 2027 and March 2028. As a result, our policy rate path remains below market 
pricing, including on a probability-weighted basis.

Risks from a French Budget Crisis 
An intensification of French budget tensions is the main downside risk to our outlook. 
French bonds could already come under stress before the elections if presidential 
candidates do not express sufficient concern regarding the fiscal outlook. Markets would 
probably react negatively to far-left leader Mélenchon progressing to the second round 
of the presidential elections, even though polls suggest he would then be very likely to 
lose against far-right leader Marine Le Pen. Investors could also question Le Pen’s 
commitment to reducing the deficit if she becomes President—as polls currently suggest 
she will—and wins a parliamentary majority. In that respect, we noted that her recent 
economic proposals would likely face significant implementation challenges, as they are 
based on optimistic growth assumptions and include costly measures. Finally, if no 
parliamentary majority emerges after the next elections—to which our model attributes 
a 20% probability— bond markets would likely react negatively to the prospect of 
continued high deficits, political uncertainty and low growth. As a result, we expect 
French government bonds to remain under pressure.

Exhibit 13: Our ECB Views Remain Dovish Compared with Market Pricing

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, ECB
13 November 2026   9
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 10

An escalation of French budget tensions could spill over into other high-debt bond 
markets (Exhibit 15). Fiscal imbalances are elevated, interest rates are high, and debt 
dynamics look concerning in several countries (including Italy, Belgium, Austria and 
Finland). Financial-market vulnerabilities also persist through sovereign exposures and 
non-bank leverage. Upcoming elections in Italy may complicate the fiscal adjustment 
required, while Spain’s fiscal outlook remains encouraging (despite the November 29 
election). Also, failure to agree on the 2028-35 EU Budget could bring into question 
fiscal cohesion.

That said, the Euro area today is stronger in several important respects than during the 
European sovereign debt crisis (Exhibit 16). Nominal growth is materially higher, 
household finances are more robust, external imbalances are smaller, and banks—
especially in Southern Europe—are much healthier. The Euro area has also strengthened 
its institutional architecture through the ESM and greater common EU issuance.

Exhibit 14: : The Risk of a French Budget Crisis

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Polymarket

Exhibit 15: European Sovereign Risk is Back in Focus

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, IMF
13 November 2026   10
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 11

Moreover, the ECB could activate its anti-fragmentation tools in the event of extreme 
bond market pressures (Exhibit 17), including the Transmission Protection Instrument 
(TPI). But our analysis suggests that ECB intervention would likely require significantly 
more intense and broad-based sovereign stress, especially with inflation well above 
target. In particular, our new index—constructed to predict ECB bond market 
interventions—suggests that fragmentation risk remains limited despite the recent 
widening in spreads.

Structural Challenges and Opportunities  
Europe continues to struggle with several structural headwinds. The Euro area has lost 
international competitiveness and faces rising competition with China, which is likely to 
act as a steady headwind and push the EU towards a more assertive trade stance. Also, 
Europe has underinvested in growth sectors, offers a less attractive business

Exhibit 16: The Euro Area is Stronger than in 2011-15

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics

Exhibit 17: Fragmentation Risk Remains Limited

Source: Goldman Sachs Global Investment Research, Haver Analytics, Polymarket
13 November 2026   11
Goldman Sachs European Economics Analyst

### Page 12

environment than its DM peers and lacks cross-country harmonisation in regulation. 
Given a significant demographic headwind, we see area-wide potential growth at only 
1%, and declining later in the decade.

That said, we also see significant growth opportunities through reforms, as outlined in 
the Draghi report. In particular, Europe could improve its economic performance 
through well-targeted public investment (including defence), leadership in growth 
industries (such as pharmaceuticals and green technologies) and widespread adoption 
of AI. Estimates also point to significant growth upside from financial market reform 
(building on Germany’s pension reform) and further integration of the internal market 
(including EU Inc to support firm creation).

While the upcoming national elections will likely complicate major progress in coming 
months, it remains essential that policymakers push reforms that lead to a lasting 
improvement in Europe’s economic performance. We will, in particular, monitor efforts

Spain's monthly pay rose faster at the bottom than across
much of the middle, though the largest gains were
concentrated near the top. The political parties diﬀer on how
to raise incomes. Some favour a higher minimum wage and
shorter working hours; others emphasise tax cuts and hiring
incentives, or propose deregulation and tighter access to
beneﬁts
Pablo Muylle
Ahead of the election on 29 November, Spain's political parties are oﬀering
diﬀerent ways to increase pay

Between 2023 and 2025, average gross monthly pay rose by 10%
in Spain’s lowest wage decile, compared with around 7% to 8%
across deciles two to six. The three highest deciles recorded gains of
11% to 12%.
Over the longer period from 2019, the bottom decile recorded the
strongest pay growth. Average gross monthly pay rose by about
33%, compared with 23% in the ﬁfth decile and 27% in the top
decile. Spain’s sharp minimum-wage increases are likely to have

Faster growth has not removed the large diﬀerences in pay. In
2025, employees in the lowest overall wage decile earned an

average of €709 per month, compared with €1,970 in the ﬁfth
decile and €5,983 in the 10th.
With Spain voting on 29 November, the election programmes oﬀer
three diﬀerent approaches to raising household incomes. The
Spanish Socialist Workers' Party (PSOE) wants to continue raising
statutory wages, reduce working time and expand social support.
Partido Popular places more weight on tax relief, incentives for
stable employment and refundable family beneﬁts. Vox proposes

broader tax cuts, less labour regulation and tighter eligibility rules
for social assistance.

### Page 3

Monthly earnings depend on working time as well as wage rates. In
2025, 86.3% of employees in the lowest monthly-wage decile
worked part-time. The share fell to 32.2% in the second decile and
to around 9.5% and 7.5% in the third and fourth deciles,
respectively. Part-time employment was uncommon from the
middle of the distribution onward.
Temporary contracts followed a similar, though less extreme,
pattern. They accounted for 32.7% of employees in the ﬁrst decile

and 18.9% in the second, compared with 3.9% in the top decile.
Employees with a foreign nationality represented more than one-
ﬁfth of the ﬁrst three deciles: 20.9%, 24.5%, and 25.0%,
respectively.
Part-time and temporary work are
concentrated in lower wage deciles
Share of employees in each overall wage decile, 2025 (%)
Source: INE, ING Economic Research
These ﬁgures put the minimum wage debate into perspective. A
higher wage ﬂoor can raise hourly or full-time-equivalent pay, but it

cannot guarantee enough paid hours. Someone working a short or
irregular schedule can remain on a low monthly income even when
the statutory rate rises. Contract stability, predictable schedules
and access to full-time work therefore matter alongside the wage
ﬂoor.

### Page 4

The programmes rely on diﬀerent combinations of wage regulation,
working-time rules, tax reductions, cash beneﬁts and public
services.
PSOE would continue the wage-led
approach
PSOE would continue using the minimum wage as part of a
broader labour market strategy. Since 2018, the monthly rate has
risen from €736 to €1,221, paid 14 times a year. The programme
would place the minimum wage above 60% of the average wage in
law, with future adjustments reﬂecting prices, wages, productivity

and workers’ needs.
Spain has already moved substantially towards a higher wage
ﬂoor. The minimum wage rose from 41.0% of the median full-time
wage in 2018 to 55.3% in 2025, an increase of more than 14
percentage points. France remained above 60%, while Germany
and the Netherlands recorded much smaller increases.
Spain’s minimum wage has moved much
closer to the median wage

proposed legal benchmark refers to the average wage, so the
measures are not directly comparable. Both show that further

### Page 5

increases would start from a much higher relative level than in
2018. Low-paid employees have recorded the strongest wage
growth while employment has continued to rise, but additional
increases may be harder for businesses with low productivity or
narrow margins.
PSOE would also reduce the standard working week to 37.5 hours
without loss of pay and encourage collective bargaining towards 35
hours. Transitional arrangements would support smaller
companies, but the combination of higher wages and shorter

hours would still raise hourly labour costs. Firms would need to
respond through higher productivity, lower margins, higher prices
or changes in employment and hours.
The party also wants employers to justify why positions are not
oﬀered on a full-time basis and would treat contracts covering
more than 80% of a standard schedule as full-time. This targets an
important source of low monthly earnings: insuﬃcient paid hours.
Beyond the labour market, PSOE proposes more support for lower-

income families through a progressive child beneﬁt, a refundable
child tax credit and wider access to childcare and care services.
These measures could reach households that receive little from
conventional tax allowances, but would also create permanent
ﬁscal costs.
Partido Popular would focus more on
disposable income
Partido Popular focuses less on statutory wage targets or shorter
working hours and more on tax cuts, incentives for stable
employment and support for families.

The party proposes lower personal income tax rates for low and
middle-income households, higher personal and family
allowances, and an automatic income tax exemption for anyone
earning the minimum wage. Tax thresholds would again be
adjusted for inﬂation. Workers and self-employed people aged 18
to 35 would receive a four-year tax reduction, beginning with a full
exemption in the ﬁrst year and declining thereafter.

### Page 6

Tax reductions can increase take-home pay without adding directly
to employers’ wage bills. They may therefore carry less immediate
employment risk than another large increase in the wage ﬂoor.
Their reach is weaker at the bottom, however. Workers whose
earnings already fall below the income-tax threshold gain little
from a conventional tax cut. This includes many people with short
working hours.
Partido Popular partly addresses this through refundable family

support. Existing deductions would be combined into a monthly
payment available even when a household’s tax liability was too
low to use the full deduction. The programme also proposes free
education from birth to age three, more ﬂexible arrangements for
caregivers and a deduction covering 40% of professional care
expenses for an elderly dependent relative.
Employers would pay lower unemployment contributions when
the stability of their workforce exceeded the average in their sector.

New self-employed workers would pay no social security
contributions during their ﬁrst year, with a possible second year for
those earning below the minimum wage.
The distributional eﬀect would depend on the balance between
conventional tax relief, which oﬀers less to people with little taxable
income, and refundable support that can reach poorer households.
Vox would cut taxes and narrow access to
beneﬁts
Vox also relies heavily on lower taxation. It proposes a simpler

personal income tax system, with rates adjusted for the number of
children. Self-employed workers earning below the minimum wage
would pay no social security contributions, with contributions
rising progressively above that threshold.
Vox would repeal the current government’s labour legislation, which
it argues has failed to eliminate insecure employment. The
programme promises stable jobs and higher wages, but provides
little detail on the rules that would replace the existing framework

or how wages would increase.

The party also proposes free education from birth to age three,
childcare support and stronger care services. Access would be more
restrictive, however. Spanish nationals would receive priority for
housing and various social beneﬁts, and the minimum-income
scheme would be limited to them.
Foreign nationals are disproportionately represented in the lower
wage deciles. These restrictions would therefore reduce support for
a group with relatively high exposure to low pay, temporary

employment and housing pressure.
The choice concerns instruments, recipients
and ﬁnancing
Gross wages are only one part of household living standards. Tax
cuts can raise net earnings but oﬀer little to people with low taxable
income, while refundable beneﬁts and public services can reach
those households more directly.
The programmes follow a clear left-right divide. PSOE relies on wage
regulation, shorter hours, employment protection and public
provision. Partido Popular emphasises tax relief, refundable family

support and employer incentives. Vox combines broader tax cuts
and less labour market regulation with tighter access to beneﬁts.
Ahead of the election on 29 November, most polls point to a
parliamentary majority for Partido Popular and Vox. If that
produces a government, policy would probably shift towards tax
relief, employer incentives and greater labour-market ﬂexibility. The
extent of the change would depend on the seat distribution and
Vox’s inﬂuence.

Spain has shown that strong wage growth at the bottom can
coexist with rising employment. Repeating that performance may
be harder from today’s higher wage ﬂoor, especially when many
low-paid employees lack access to suﬃcient working hours or
stable contracts. Higher wage ﬂoors and shorter working weeks
carry risks for low-productivity employers, while conventional tax
cuts often miss the lowest earners. The challenge for the next

Document 408: The Big Picture_ Plunge Protection

Report source: researchmail轉寄

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: The Big Picture: Plunge Protection
Date:    6 hours ago
The Big Picture: Plunge Protection

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
The Big Picture: Plunge
Protection
By Ed Yardeni • 23 Nov 2026
View in browser
Photo by Ma Fo / Unsplash

I. Finding Support
The S&P 500 may be finding support around 7,000, which marked the
index's record high earlier this year, just before the Middle East war
started (chart). If so, the S&P 500 may have bottomed on Friday at
6,910.39. Yet another V-shaped rebound is also possible. Nevertheless,
we did lower our year-end target for the second month in a row on
Saturday to 7,500 from 7,900. We are still using $425 per share as our
2027 forward earnings estimate at year-end 2026. But we are lowering our

### Page 2

forward P/E estimate from 18.6 to 17.6 to reflect ongoing jitters about the
AI trade.
We still view the post-midterms selloff as a buying opportunity. We
currently project the S&P 500 will rise to 8,500 to 9,000 by the end of
2027.
We should mention that if support doesn't hold at 7,000, the next level is
the low of 6,343.72 on March 30 of this year.
The Nasdaq low on March 30, 2025 was 20,794.64.
II. Historical Perspective

### Page 3

We view the recent S&P 500 selloff as another correction in the bull
market that started on October 12, 2022 (chart).
The S&P 500 remains in a significant uptrend that began in March 2009,
marking the trough of the bear market of the Great Financial Crisis (chart).
The same holds for the Nasdaq (chart).

### Page 4

The Nasdaq has been outperforming the S&P 500 since the late 1970s
(chart).
III. Earnings
Analysts' consensus earnings estimates have been rising significantly for
2026, 2027, and 2028 since the start of this year (chart). This is a very
unusual trifecta. The previous trifecta was more muted in 2024, 2025, and
2026. The question is whether analysts' current projections are irrationally
exuberant. They might be, but we don't expect significant downward

revisions like those during the Tech Wreck of the early 2000s. Importantly,
we also don't expect a recession over the rest of the Roaring 2020s.

### Page 5

The strong upward revisions in analysts' earnings estimates for 2026-2028
are pushing S&P 500 forward earnings to record highs. That's likely to
continue if the rest of the decade remains recession-free. However, the
recent ascent in forward earnings is likely to slow, especially if industry
analysts cut their outlook for the volatile S&P 500 Semiconductor industry.
The y/y growth rate of S&P 500 forward earnings is highly correlated with
the comparable growth rate in nominal GDP (chart). The widespread

notion that the stock market is not the economy is wrong. The economy
obviously drives corporate earnings. Both are performing well.

### Page 6

IV. 2026 Versus Past 10 Years
For the S&P 500, 2026 has been much more volatile than the past 10-
year average (chart).
The same can be said for the Nasdaq (chart).

### Page 7

V. Valuation
The S&P 500 forward P/E is now lower than it was on Liberation Day 2025
(chart). It is well below the peaks of the Tech Wreck in the early 2000s.
We don't see much more downside barring a recession.
The forward P/E of the S&P 500 Growth composite has plunged since
mid-2025 (chart).

### Page 8

Growth is very cheap. The ratio of the forward P/Es of S&P 500 Growth
and Value is down to 1.03, the lowest since the bear market of the Great
Financial Crisis (chart).

Document 410: The ECB’s new team faces a testing start — with the skills to match (2026-11-23)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The ECB’s new team faces a testing start — with the skills to match (2026-11-23).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-11-23
Pages: 2
Status: ok

### Page 1

The bank
for a changing
world
3
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.43 / 23 November 2026EDITORIAL
THE ECB’S NEW TEAM FACES A TESTING START — WITH THE SKILLS TO MATCH
European leaders are set to agree on a package deal at their 26–27 November meeting for the three ECB Board seats falling
vacant in the first five months of 2027. Individually, they differ markedly from their predecessors. Collectively, they bring
a remarkably similar mix of skills. Policy preferences may be another matter. In any case, they will need to hit the ground

running. Top priorities: communications, crisis management, soft landing, balance sheet, policy-making upgrades, and role
of the euro.
MORE CONTINUITY THAN MEETS THE EYES, WITH A BONUS
Based on the nominations filed by last Friday’s deadline, EU leaders
are expected to appoint Spain’s Pablo Hernández de Cos to replace
Christine Lagarde as President in February; France’s Laurence Boone
to take over market operations from Isabel Schnabel in January; and

Germany’s Markus Brunnermeier to succeed Philip Lane as chief eco-
nomist in May.
The incoming Board members share their predecessors’ international
standing and commitment to Europe and the euro. Beyond that, the in-
dividual differences are striking. Yet together they offer the same blend
of monetary policy expertise, academic financial economics and politi-
cal nous. Monetary experience comes not from the chief economist this
time, but from Hernandez de Cos, a former Bank of Spain governor who

now heads the Bank for International Settlements, the “bank of central
banks”. Brunnermeier brings his expertise as a Princeton professor;
Boone her experience as a presidential “sherpa”, OECD chief economist
and minister for Europe. Her stints at Axa and several international
investment banks also bring an insider’s knowledge of capital markets
that the Board has lacked since Mario Draghi. All three were involved,
if not in frontline roles, in managing the Eurozone crises of the past

decade. The essential skills are there.
WILL THE ECB LEAN MORE DOVISH?
A more dovish ECB is not a foregone conclusion. Judging by his record,
I expect the new President to seek the same “owl” label as his prede-
cessor: neither dove nor hawk. Boone is likely to be as dovish as Lane.
Brunnermeier, however, is hardly a hawk — a marked contrast with the
outgoing German Board member, Schnabel. The Governing Council’s
hawks will need another champion. They will surely find at least one:

perhaps Vice-President Boris Vujcic, who succeeded Luis de Guindos
last June, or a new national central bank governor. Nature abhors a
vacuum, in ornithology as elsewhere.
NO SMOOTH SAILING AHEAD FOR THE NEW CREW
Thorny challenges await:
First, inflation is running at twice the ECB’s target, and has probably
not peaked, even if oil prices go no higher. Growth has proved sur-
prisingly resilient to rate rises, so the ever-feared trade-off between
fighting inflation and sustaining growth has yet to materialise. It will

sharpen if inflation proves stickier than expected.
Second, markets have been unsettled for weeks by fears of an AI
bubble, and of an ever-ascending path for French public debt, which
the coming spring elections could worsen. The risk premium on French
debt has hovered around twice its level at the start of the year, threate-
ning contagion to other high debt Eurozone sovereigns, notably Italy,
Belgium, and Spain. Market moves have remained orderly, and finan-

cial conditions have tightened only moderately thus far. But that may
not last.
Third, cyber disruptions, whether driven by AI agents or other sources,
have become alarmingly more frequent in recent weeks. They could
rattle the financial system, in the Eurozone or globally, at any time.
THE NEW LEADERSHIP’S PRIORITIES
The first few days
Find a sound communications footing. Many a central bank leader ce-
lebrated as a tough act to follow upon leaving office caused unintended

market volatility early in their tenure. Little prepares for the power of
every word to move markets, or for an off-the-cuff remark to undo the
effects of a carefully drafted statement. Sometimes, the lessons can be
learnt without serious consequences. Now is not such a time.
The first few weeks
- Build a new modus operandi. Each of Hernández de Cos’s prede-
cessors brought a different leadership style and skill set, and faced
different personal dynamics on the Board and Governing Council. Each

eventually made it work. That cannot be taken for granted. Efficient, ef-
fective, and well-understood decision-making — for which the current
ECB deserves high marks — takes work. With two-thirds of the Board
changing in less than a year, establishing an effective way of working
will be paramount. Fortunately, Hernandez de Cos wins high praise
from those involved in the many central banking committees he has
chaired over the years.
- Take ownership of crisis management playbooks — before a crisis

hits. Several, no doubt, exist already. The new President and Board
members will want to be comfortable with them and may add their
own touches. No crisis follows a script, but thinking through the op-
tions, trade-offs, and contingencies ahead of time can save precious
time and avert mistakes.
The first few months
- Deliver a soft landing. Both the ECB’s central forecast and econo-
mists’ consensus envisage Eurozone inflation returning close to target

by late 2027 or early 2028, with growth holding at 1–1.5%. The risks
outlined above mean that this outcome is far from assured. The ECB
will need to stay nimble.
- Finalise the balance sheet strategy. How large should the ECB’s ba-
lance sheet be in steady state, and which instruments should manage
systemic liquidity? The 2025 Strategy Review left these questions open:
there was broad agreement that unwinding crisis-era asset purchases
could stay on autopilot for a while. That phase is nearing its end. Better

to have a plan before problems arise, not least given the fraught mar-
ket environment that has settled in.

### Page 2

4
economic-research.bnpparibas.comEcoWeek 26.43 / 23 November 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
- Consider the combined effect of monetary, balance sheet and macro-
prudential policies. A coherent, comprehensive approach would be a
major step forward and improve the odds of a soft landing. The new
Board is uniquely qualified to take it: for the first time, it will have deep
expertise and experience across all these dimensions, starting with the
President and Vice-President.

Longer term
- Understand AI’s economic effects on aggregate demand, labour mar-
kets and productivity, and the implications for monetary policy. Ex-
cellent work on this is already under way, but the effects will evolve
rapidly. Unless policymakers anticipate them, or at least understand
them in real time, monetary policy risks missing the mark. The ECB
must also clarify which effects monetary policy can address, and which
belong with fiscal authorities, such as a rise in structural unemploy-

ment, were it to occur.
- Use AI to improve monetary policy analysis and decision-making.
Brunnermeier has been at the forefront of academic economists’ work
in this area, and should be able to play a decisive role in upgrading the
ECB’s work in this regard.
- Continue to promote the euro’s international role and its place as a
pillar of EU sovereignty. That means continuing to: advocate for pro-
gress in the architecture of the European Monetary Union; modernise

euro-based cross-border payments and remain at the frontier of sup-
port for a vibrant euro-based blockchain finance ecosystem through
the ECB’s Pontes and Appia projects.
I am confident that the new leadership is as qualified as any to deliver.
Nonetheless, their task is a daunting one. All EU citizens and corpo-
rates should wish them every success. I certainly do.
Isabelle Mateos y Lago
isabelle.mateosylago@bnpparibas.com

3
《歐元投資關鍵-法國政治風險令歐元走弱》
10月以來歐元區一連串的動盪導致歐元走弱。除了
西班牙提前大選，法國政治局勢惡化，極右派勝出
機率上升加學生示威遊行演變成各地零星暴動，經
濟下行風險升溫。法國政府提出目標將赤字占GDP
比從5.4%降至5%的方案，但反對黨已表態不支持。
目前執政黨在下議院非多數，財政政策停擺風險導
致公債殖利率大幅上升。法國10年期公債殖利率與
德國公債殖利率利差創2011年歐債危機最嚴重時期
以來最高水準。本行認為，法國右翼已放棄脫歐，
且強調財政紀律將遵守歐盟規範，法國選後議會仍

需籌組多數聯盟，財政惡化風險可控。
總經方面，歐元區經濟基本面持續轉佳。10月PMI
顯示經濟持續擴張，出口訂單連續第3個月增加，顯
示外需逐步改善。10月PMI對應歐元區GDP季增率
約0.3%，為近3年相對穩健的季度表現。通膨方面，
能源成本持續上升，企業為維持獲利而調升售價，
高利率逐漸抑制需求。德國央行最新通膨報告顯示，
目前複製2021-22年高通膨階段的可能性不大，但
通膨風險明顯偏向上行。展望後續，ECB 10月會議
維持利率不變，但通膨的僵固性將支持12月升息，
歐洲與美利差預期小幅收斂。基本面繼續支持ECB
升息的環境下，預計美歐利差收斂將提供歐元支撐，
短線政治風險提供逢低布局時機，4Q26目標價1.19。

《英鎊投資關鍵-英國經濟動能逐漸改善》
10月經濟數據方面，英國PMI初值顯示經濟動能持

創2024年9月以來新高，帶動企業活動加速擴張，

原物料與運輸成本上揚所帶動，且中東衝突與供應
鏈干擾延長交貨時間。企業對一年景氣展望樂觀，
但程度仍低於長期平均。經濟韌性增強及通膨壓力
居高不下，使英國央行將維持在貨幣緊縮的路徑，

且2027年進一步升息的可能性上升。對於英鎊的前
景逐漸樂觀，英鎊兌美元年底目標1.34。

《瑞郎投資關鍵-瑞士景氣連續第7個月擴張》
瑞士9月製造業PMI下滑1.8點至55.3，但連續第7個
月維持在擴張區間。本次下滑主因產出、供應商交貨
時間及就業分項走弱。訂單積壓指數則持穩於57.0，
顯示需求仍具韌性，但企業持續去化庫存，原材料與
成品庫存皆低於50。採購價格指數升至70，反映電
子零組件及原物料價格上漲，中東地緣政治風險及AI
熱潮亦推升成本壓力。服務業PMI由58.3升至59.8，
維持強勁成長，新訂單與商業活動改善，就業指數升
至2025年4月以來高點。採購價格及銷售價格同步明
顯上升，顯示服務業通膨壓力有增溫跡象。整體而言，

瑞士通膨及成長前景溫和，央行並未因地緣政治風險
而強調通膨壓力，利差擴大為瑞郎走弱主因。央行對
升息態度保守，外需回溫與製造業改善正帶動瑞士經
濟展望持續好轉。4Q26美元兌瑞郎維持目標價0.8。

金融環境依然寬鬆的觀點，這表明與聯準會和歐洲央
行相比，日本央行將更傾向於進一步升息。

走強，但升幅未如7月及9月政府干預匯市之規模。

政府對於穩定匯市的決心。財務大臣片山表示，美元
兌日圓匯率較先前接近164時有所改善，當局將對無

• 美元兌日圓4Q26目標價160
高。
政策方面，近期高市早苗政策面將從12月起，將汽
油補貼計畫下的基準汽油價格每月提高5日圓，但從
12月起恢復電力和天然氣補貼。整體而言，隨地緣
政治風險延續，政府控制汽油價格的措施將演變成一
場持久戰，政府將尋求透過動用儲備金和分階段縮減
汽油補貼來減輕財政負擔，但油價長期維持在高點將
繼續對財政帶來壓力。本行認為，日本財政政策及擴
大支出施政方針對日圓不利，惟美元兌日圓160為政
府可容忍的上緣，美元兌日圓4Q26目標價維持160。

保険業界の規制改革とインド経済
インド政府は、過去四半世紀にわたり国民生活の安定と金融仲介機能の強化に向けて保険市場の自由化を
推進してきたが、ここにきて消費者保護強化に向けた規制強化にかじを切ろうとしている。
■規制緩和を受けてインドの保険市場は急成長
インド政府は、国民生活の安定と金融仲介機
能の強化に向けて、保険市場の発展を推進して
きた。この背景としては、以下の 2 点を指摘で
きる。
第 1 に、インドでは社会保障制度が整備途上
にある。そのため、予期せぬ疾病や事故をきっ
かけとする世帯所得の急減リスクを抑制するう
えで、医療保険や生命保険への加入が極めて重
要なセーフティネットとなる。
第 2 に、持続的なインド経済の発展のために
は、預入期間の短い銀行預金とは別に、インフ
ラ整備や産業育成の原動力となる長期かつ安定
的な国内資本の蓄積が必要である。保険会社が

集めて運用する巨額の資金は、まさにこの長期
資金供給の担い手として期待されてきた。
これらの目的の達成に向け、インド政府は過
去四半世紀にわたって段階的な規制緩和を進め
てきた（右上表）。2000 年の民間企業や外資企
業の参入自由化を皮切りに、2002 年のバンカシ
ュアランス（銀行窓口での保険販売）解禁、外
資出資比率上限の引き上げなどを進めた。
こうした一連の規制緩和によって多数の民
間・外国企業が参入し、市場競争が活性化した。
日本からも日本生命や東京海上グループを含め、
複数の保険会社が参入し、対印投資を牽引した。
市場参入や販売に関わる規制緩和に加え、中
間所得層の拡大、家計における金融リテラシー
の向上、そして保険サービスのデジタル化も相
まって、インドの保険市場は飛躍的な拡大を遂
げた。保険料収入は経済成長率を上回るペースで拡大し、近年の保険会社の運用資産残高は GDP
の約 4 分の 1 に達している（右下図）。
インド政府は、独立 100 周年を迎える 2047 年までに全国民が自らのリスクに適した保険に手

ごろな価格でアクセスできる「Insurance for All by 2047」の実現を目標に掲げており、今後もイ
ンドの保険市場は基本的に底堅い成長をたどると見込まれる。

(kumagai.shotaro@jri.co.jp）

JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
2
■当局が規制強化に舵を切る理由
一方で、急激な市場拡大の副作用として、構造
的な問題も顕在化している。最大の課題は保険販
売コストの高騰と不適切販売の横行である。保険
販売競争が激化するなか、保険会社が商業銀行や
ノンバンクといった販売代理店に対して支払う
手数料・報酬は、新規契約保険料を大幅に上回る
ペースで増加しており（右上図）、それが新規保
険料の上昇や将来の保険料支払いの減少につな
がることへの懸念が広がった。また、顧客のニー
ズに合致しない複雑な商品の強引な販売や、商品
に関する説明不足も社会問題化した。
こうした状況を重くみた IRDAI（イン
ド保険規制開発局）は、市場の健全性を
維持すべく、足元で消費者保護を主眼と
した規制強化を検討している。2026 年 9
月に公表した改革案では、①販売代理店
への報酬構造の見直し、②保険会社の事
業費・管理費に対する規制厳格化、③販

売手法に対する規制厳格化、④コンプラ
イアンスの徹底に向けた流通チャネルの
合理化、⑤監査や情報公開制度の厳格化、
などの方向性が示された（右下表）。改
革案に関する意見を広く募ったうえで、
2027 年前半に新たな制度を導入するとし
ている。
一連の規制強化の影響は、プラス面と
マイナス面に分けられる。まず、プラス
面としては、過度なインセンティブ競争
や不適切な販売が抑制されることで消費
者保護が大幅に強化され、保険市場の信
頼性と持続的発展の基盤が強化される点が挙げられる。
他方、マイナス面として懸念されるのは、移行期における副作用である。販売規制の厳格化に
伴い、保険会社および販売代理店である金融機関の収益拡大ペースの一時的な鈍化は避けられな
い。さらに、保険会社や金融機関がインドの金融市場における主たる投資主体であることを勘案
すると、規制強化が株式・債券価格に対して下押し圧力をもたらす公算が大きい。
保険市場のこうした変化は、今後のインド経済を展望するうえで重要な視点を提供する。すな

わち、保険業界に限らず多くの産業において、自由化による市場の急成長は様々な問題を生じさ
せ得るものの、そうした事態に対する規制の再設計・適正化こそが、新たな発展の礎になるとい
う点である。その嚆矢として保険をめぐる規制の行方が注目される。

   3
専門人材の渡航制限が中国の成長を下押す恐れ 
中国政府は 9 月、新たな出入国管理規定を施行した。技術流出を防ぐための人材の囲い込みは、海外の知
識を取り込む機会を狭め、中長期的な経済成長を下押しする恐れがある。

■海外渡航制限の対象者を拡大 
中国政府は 9 月 15 日、出入国の条件や審査手
続きを具体化する新規定を施行した。新規定では、
輸出管理・技術輸出入の規則に違反し、国家の産
業・技術の安全を脅かす可能性がある中国国民に
対して、商務部などが出国禁止を決定できる旨が
明記された（右表）。 
中国では、2013 年に施行された出境入境管理
法に基づき、刑事事件の被告人・被疑者や、未解
決の民事事件について裁判所が出国を認めない
者などは、出国制限の対象となっていた。また、
中国国民については、国家の安全と利益を損なう
恐れがあることを理由に、関係当局が出国を認め
ない場合も規定されていた。出国禁止とは別に、
国家機密を扱う人員や公的部門の幹部らに対し、旅券の集中保管や渡航の事前承認による管理も
行われてきた。今回の新規定では、経済安全保障面での出国制限が明確化された形となる。 
こうしたなか、9 月末には、AI・半導体分野の重要人材に課されていた海外渡航制限が、配偶
者や子供などの家族にも拡大されたと報じられた。報道によれば、重要人材には、著名な AI スタ
ートアップ企業の創業者、大手民間企業の主要幹部、中核的な研究開発人材などが含まれる。一
律の出国禁止ではないものの、対象者とその家族は、短期の私的旅行や出張であっても、出国に
当局の許可が必要とされる。 
今回の規制強化の背景には、米中間の技術競争があると考えられる。生成 AI などの重要技術を
巡る競争が激しくなるなか、中国政府は、技術だけでなく、それを生み出す研究者や技術者も戦
略的な資産として重視しているとみられる。家族の渡航も管理することで、本人の海外移住や研
究開発拠点の移転を抑制する狙いがあると考えられる。今回の報道で注目されるのは、公的部門
に限らず、民間企業の重要人材やその家族にも管理が及んでいる点である。今回のような人材移
動の制約は、これまで中国の成長を支えてきた技術吸収と生産性向上のメカニズムを揺るがす恐
れがある。 
■市場開放と技術吸収に支えられた中国の生産性上昇 
経済成長に対して、生産性は大きな影響力を持つ。一国経済の生産効率を示す全要素生産性
（Total Factor Productivity, TFP）は、資本や労働の投入量だけでは説明できない生産の効率性
を捉える推計指標であり、技術水準や資源配分の効率性などを反映する。 
中国の TFP は、長期的に上昇してきた（次頁右上図）。背景には、改革開放後の市場化や民間
企業の成長に加え、貿易・直接投資を通じて、海外の技術や経営ノウハウを取り込んできたこと
が指摘できる。加えて、農業から製造業などへの労働移動や、企業間の競争・新陳代謝も、経済
研究員 古宮 大夢 
(komiya.hiromu@jri.co.jp） 
＜新規定における中国国⺠の出国制限＞

・ 出⼊国書類の不正取得、不法出⼊
　 国による⾏政拘禁を受けた者
・ 国外で国家の安全と利益を脅かす違
　 法⾏為や犯罪⾏為を⾏った者
・ 関係当局が出国禁止を決定できる
・ 輸出管理や技術輸出⼊の規則に違
　 反し、国家の産業・技術の安全を脅
　 かす可能性がある者

犯罪⾏為など
産業・技術の
安全保障

### Page 5

ASIA MONTHLY  2026 年 11 月（No.308）
JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
4
全体の生産性向上を支えてきた。
もっとも、中国の TFP は主要国のなかでは依
然として低い水準にある（右下図）。Penn World
Table の推計によると、2023 年の中国の TFP は
米国の半分弱であり、ドイツをはじめとする欧
州主要国、韓国や日本はもとより、タイやマレ
ーシアの水準にも届いていない。背景には、非
効率な資源配分や先進的な技術・経営手法の普
及の遅れが挙げられる。国有企業への金融・制
度面での優遇、地方保護主義、企業の参入・退
出を妨げる仕組みなどが資源配分を歪め、資本
や労働が生産性の高い企業・部門で十分に活用
されていない。また、中国が電気自動車や半導
体などの一部の先端分野で高い競争力を持って
いても、そうした産業の先進的な技術・経営手
法が経済の大宗を担うサービス業や中小企業な
どには普及していないことも、全体の生産性向
上を制約する要因となっている。

■技術の囲い込みが中長期的に成長を阻害
技術流出を防ぐための人材の囲い込みは、生
産性の先進国へのキャッチアップを一層遅らせ
る恐れがある。専門人材の渡航制限には、国内
の研究開発人材を確保し、既存の技術的優位を
守る側面がある一方、海外の知識を吸収したり、
新たな技術を海外と共同で生み出したりする機会を狭める可能性がある。技術やノウハウの移転
は、論文や設計図といった「形式知」の共有だけで完結するものではなく、対面での議論や共同
作業、生産現場での経験を通じて共有される「暗黙知」が極めて重要である。渡航管理によって
国際学会への参加や共同研究、海外企業との技術交流が遅延・見送りとなれば、こうした知識を
吸収する機会が失われ、技術革新や生産工程の改善が遅れる恐れがある。さらに、渡航許可の取
得に時間がかかったり、許可の見通しが立ちにくくなったりすれば、技術者の海外派遣や国際的
な人材配置の障害にもなる。
今回施行された新規定は外国人の入国管理も対象としており、出入国管理の運用への不透明感

が高まれば、海外からの高度人材の招聘や定着にとっても障壁となる。すでに非効率な資源配分
や先進的な知見の普及の遅れが中国経済の生産性向上の重石となっているなか、海外との人的交
流まで制約されれば、知識の吸収・創出と、その活用がさらに滞る可能性がある。影響の大きさ
は対象範囲や実際の運用に左右されるものの、技術保護を過度に重視した人材の交流や渡航に関
する制限は中長期的に TFP の伸びを鈍らせ、中国経済の潜在成長率を下押しする逆風となり得る。

5
台 湾 AI ブームを背景に高成長が継続
■足元は内外需ともに好調
台湾景気は、内外需に支えられて拡大している。
外需の面では、世界的な AI 需要の高まりを受け
て米国向けの半導体や AI サーバーの輸出が急増
しており、輸出全体は前年同月比＋50％程度で推
移している。一方で、停滞が指摘されていた AI
ブームの恩恵を受けにくいその他製品の輸出も、
足元では 2 桁の伸びを見せている（右上図）。た
だし、その主因は輸出品の価格上昇である。実際、
それら製品を製造する産業の生産活動は横ばい
にとどまっており、本格的な回復とは言えない状
況である。
個人消費は、良好な雇用・所得環境に支えられ
て好調に推移している。製造業では、AI ブームに
押し上げられている電子部品産業のほか、幅広い
製造業において高水準の賃上げが行われている。
さらに、株価の上昇による資産効果も消費を押し

上げており、台湾の代表的な株価指数である加権
指数は、年初から 10 月初旬までに＋60％上昇し
た。これらを背景に、小売売上高は、主に生活必
需品を販売する一般店、嗜好品を多く扱う専門店ともに高水準の伸びを維持している（右下図）。
投資も拡大している。AI ブームを背景に、電子部品産業による生産能力の増強投資が活発化し
ており、同産業が台湾全体の設備投資や工場建設増加の 9 割以上を占めている。
■2026 年の実質 GDP 成長率は＋10％超へ
先行きの台湾経済を展望すると、輸出と投資を両輪とする景気拡大が続き、2026 年の実質 GDP
成長率は＋11.1％に達すると予想する。2 桁の成長率は、16 年ぶりとなる。
輸出は引き続き増加する見込みである。海外のデータセンター投資の拡大を背景に、半導体や
サーバーの輸出は好調を維持すると予想する。
投資も好調を維持する見通しである。半導体や AI サーバーのメーカーは相次いで今年の設備投
資計画を上方修正しているほか、関連部材の製造業などでも能力増強投資が拡大している。

台湾経済の主な下振れリスクは、AI 投資の収益性への懸念が高まり、AI ブームが調整局面を迎
えることである。AI サーバーに用いられる GPU は 3～6 年程度で陳腐化する可能性があり、買い
替えによる投資負担の増大が、収益性を悪化させるとの指摘もある。仮に世界の AI インフラ投資
のペースが鈍化すれば、AI 関連偏重の台湾の輸出と投資を大きく下押しすると考えられる。
また、物価高による消費下押しもリスク要因である。足元の消費者物価の前年同月比は、中央
銀行の警戒ラインである＋2％を上回って推移している。①エネルギー価格の高止まり、②パソコ
ンや携帯電話などの半導体搭載製品の価格高騰、③積極的な賃上げを背景とするサービス価格の
上昇など、足元で見られるインフレ圧力の先行きに注意が必要である。
研究員 今川 冬馬（imagawa.thoma@jri.co.jp）

6
フィリピン インフレは一段と加速
■物価高が景気を下押し
フィリピン景気は、外需は好調に推移している
ものの、内需の低迷を補いきれない状況が続いて
いる。
外需の面では、8 月の財輸出が前年同月比＋
27.8％と、前月の同＋10.8％から大幅に加速した
（右上図）。世界的な AI 関連需要の拡大を背景
に、電子機器・部品がけん引役となっている。輸
出が極めて好調であることから、7 月まで悪化し
ていた貿易収支にも、足元では改善の兆しがみら
れる。
これに対し、内需は依然として力強さを欠いて
いる。中東危機を背景とするエネルギー価格の高
止まりに加え、台風や豪雨による食品価格の上昇
が、家計の実質購買力を圧迫している。9 月の消
費者物価指数（CPI）は同＋7.2％と、8 月の同＋
6.1％から伸びが加速し、中銀の目標レンジ上限
である＋4％を大きく上回った（右下図）。
価格転嫁の動きは幅広い財・サービスに及んで

おり、変動の大きい食品とエネルギーを除くコア
CPI も同＋4.7％と、前月の同＋4.1％から上昇し
ている。背景には、ペソ安に伴う輸入コストの上
昇が挙げられる。フィリピン・ペソは 2 月末から本稿執筆時点までに、対ドルで▲9.2％下落した。
こうしたなか、中銀はインフレ抑制を優先し、4 月以降、3 会合連続で利上げを実施し、政策金
利は 5.0％の高水準となっている。インフレ率が目標レンジを大幅に上回るなか、今後も金融引き
締め姿勢を維持するとみられる。
■公共投資の回復に遅れ
内需の低迷は家計部門にとどまらない。昨年の洪水対策事業を巡る汚職問題を受け、政府が事
業点検や支払い審査を厳格化したため、4～6 月期の公共投資は前年に比べて大幅に減少した。足
元でもインフラ関連支出の回復は鈍く、景気の下押し要因となっている。10 月には、国会議員や
公共事業道路省職員、建設業者といった関係者の訴追が始まるなど、汚職事件を巡る捜査が本格
化している。こうした一連の動きは、中長期的に公共事業の健全化につながる一方、短期的には

工事の再開を遅らせ、投資を冷え込ませるとみられる。
先行き、堅調な AI 関連需要を背景に電子機器・部品の輸出は底堅く推移するとみられる一方、
内需は引き続き力強さを欠く見通しである。公共投資の停滞に加え、インフレ高進による実質購
買力の低下や、政策金利の高止まりによる借入コストの上昇が、個人消費や民間投資を抑制する
とみられる。
研究員 呉 子婧（wu.zijing@jri.co.jp）

ASIA MONTHLY 2026 年 11 月（No.308）
JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
7
タ イ 輸出主導で回復も、消費に弱さ
■個人消費の回復は緩慢
タイ景気は、AI・テクノロジー関連需要の拡
大を背景に輸出と民間投資が想定を上回る一方、
個人消費は力強さを欠くなど、需要項目によっ
て勢いにばらつきが見られる。
輸出は高い伸びを維持している。8 月の財輸出
は前年同月比＋24.3％と、前月の同＋23.9％から
伸びが加速した。世界的な AI 関連需要の拡大を
受けて、電子機器・部品が輸出をけん引してい
る。民間投資も増勢を強めている。データセン
ターや電子・半導体関連設備への投資拡大が主因となっている。
これに対し、個人消費の回復は緩慢である。政府による一連の消費支援策を受け、ホテル・レ
ストランなどのサービス消費や耐久財支出は増加したものの、家計の節約志向は依然として根強
い。9 月の消費者信頼感指数は 52.1 と、中立水準の 100 を大きく下回り、本格的な改善には至っ

ていない（右上図）。背景には、GDP 比 85％を超える家計債務の返済負担が可処分所得を圧迫し
ていることに加え、中小企業向け融資の減少により雇用・所得環境の改善が遅れ、企業部門好調
の恩恵が家計に十分波及していないことがある。
■物価上昇と洪水被害が景気の下押し要因に
こうしたなか、足元では物価上昇も家計消費
の重石となっている。9 月の消費者物価指数は前
年同月比＋2.8％と、8 月の同＋2.5％から伸びが
加速した（右下図）。中東情勢の緊迫化に伴う
エネルギー価格の高止まりと、天候不順による
生鮮食品価格の上昇が主因である。
9 月以降の洪水被害の深刻化も、さらなる景気
下押し要因となっている。タイ防災減災局
（DDPM）は 10 月 4 日、洪水の影響が約 120
万世帯、326 万人以上に及んだと発表した。洪
水は農地や物流網にも被害をもたらしており、農産物の出荷停滞を通じて食品価格を一段と押し
上げる懸念がある。企業活動への影響も広がっている。タイ工業連盟（FTI）によると、工業部門

の損失額は 10 月初旬時点で約 60 億バーツに上り、とりわけ自動車産業では部品供給への懸念が
高まっている。被害が長期化すれば、部品不足や輸送コストの高騰を通じて企業の生産活動が下
押しされる可能性がある。さらに、家屋や設備の復旧に向けた政府支出の増加は、短期的には景
気を下支えする一方、中長期的な財政運営上の負担となる可能性もある。
以上を踏まえると、需要面と供給面の両方からの下押し圧力が強まることで、先行きの景気は
力強さを欠く展開が続く見通しである。2026 年の実質 GDP 成長率は＋1.8％と、前年の＋2.4％
から減速すると予想する。
研究員 呉 子婧（wu.zijing@jri.co.jp）

ASIA MONTHLY 2026 年 11 月（No.308）
JAPAN RESEARCH INSTITUTE
8
中 国 景気減速にようやく歯止め
■7～9 月期の成長率は持ち直し
中国景気は減速に歯止めがかかった。本年 7～
9 月期の実質 GDP は前年同期比＋4.5％と、前期
から若干持ち直した（右上図）。
押し上げの主因は、好調な輸出である。7～9
月期のドル建て輸出額は同＋28.4％と、4～6 月
期（同＋20.1％）に比べて伸びが加速した。地域
別では、ASEAN や EU 向けが好調に推移した。
米国向けも、両国の経済関係の改善の流れを受け、
前年同月比プラスが続いている。品目別では、
EV を含む自動車、AI ブームを背景とした IT 関連財がけん引した。
一方、内需は引き続き低迷している。固定資産投資は、過剰生産や不動産不況といった構造的
な下押し要因が残存し、総じて低調であった。とくに、不動産開発投資は前年同期比でマイナス
幅が拡大している。政府が製造業高度化の象徴として重視するハイテク製造業など、一部の業種

では堅調に拡大したものの、投資全体を押し上げるには至っていない。
消費は、財を中心に弱含んでいる。7～9 月期の小売売上高は同＋0.7％と、前期からほぼ横ばい
で推移した。背景には、①自動車が買い替え補助金による需要の反動減と、原油高によるガソリ
ン車需要の減退から大幅減となったこと、②不動産不況を受け、家具・建材などの売上の不振が
続いていること、の 2 点がある。旅行や外食は、9 月末の大型連休期間中は需要が増加したものの、
一過性の持ち直しに終わった。
■先行き、景気刺激策が押し上げ要因に
こうした内需の低迷を受け、政府は 9 月末以降、
追加対策を発表している（右下表）。景気浮揚効
果の観点で注目される措置としては、まず住宅ロ
ーンの利子補給が挙げられる。また、金融緩和を
通じて、ハイテク分野への投資や次世代通信網・
物流網の整備といった「六つのネットワーク」を
推進しようとしている。これらの取り組みは、住
宅関連需要の持ち直しや、ハイテク製造業とイン
フラ部門の投資拡大などをもたらし、内需を押し

上げると見込まれる。加えて、外需も当面は好調
を維持するため、10～12 月期の成長率は 7～9
月期を若干上回る見通しである。その結果、2026
年通年では政府目標（＋4.5～5.0％）をかろうじ
て達成できる見込みである。
もっとも、それを踏まえても内需は自律的な回復には至らないとみられ、政策効果が薄れる 2027
年入り後に、景気は再び減速すると予想される。
主任研究員 佐野 淳也（sano.junya@jri.co.jp）

今月の景気判断 ～持ち直している
現状
景気全般 持ち直している
生産 増加している
輸出 足踏み状態となっている
設備投資 増加している
雇用 持ち直しの動きがみられる
賃金 持ち直している
個人消費 持ち直している
住宅投資 横ばい圏で推移している
公共投資 高めの水準となっている
項目
【今月のポイント】
 東海経済は、持ち直している
 企業部門では堅調な数字が確認されるが、
家計部門は弱めの数字が散見される
 8月の生産（東海3県、季節調整値）は前月
比－0.1％と2ヵ月ぶりに減少したが、均し
てみると増加基調にある
 輸出は均してみると足踏み状態。先行きは
持ち直しを見込むが、米・イランの対立長
期化などで足踏みとなるリスクがある
 9月の小売販売額は名目で3ヵ月連続で前
年比プラスとなったが、実質では4ヵ月連続
でマイナス。一方、新車販売台数は6ヵ月
連続で前年比プラスとなった
 8月の名目賃金（愛知県）は前年比＋0.8％

と2ヵ月ぶりに増加したが、実質では同－
1.6％と2ヵ月連続で減少した

生産
8月の鉱工業生産（東海3県）は前月比－0.1％と2ヵ月ぶりに減少した。輸送機械は減少したが、電子部品・
デバイス、電気機械、汎用・生産用・業務用機械が増加した。東海の生産は均してみると増加基調にある。先
行きについては高水準での推移を見込むが、米・イランの対立長期化により下振れるリスクもある。
（出所）経済産業省「鉱工業指数」

輸出
8月の実質輸出指数（季節調整値）は、前月比＋0.5％と3ヵ月ぶりに上昇した。当社試算による9月の輸出数
量指数（季節調整値）は、前月比＋9.4％と2ヵ月ぶりに上昇した。東海の輸出は、均してみると足踏み状態と
なっている。先行きについては、生産の増加に応じて輸出も持ち直しが期待されるが、米・イランの対立長期
化により足踏みとなるリスクがある。

企業景況感（日銀短観）
日銀短観9月調査の業況判断DIは、全産業で＋22ptと6月調査(＋19pt)から改善した。製造業では、非鉄金
属、生産用機械、電気機械、自動車等が改善し、全体でも改善となる一方、非製造業では建設、小売、情報
通信などが悪化し、全体としては小幅悪化したものの、引き続き高い水準を維持している。先行き（12月）につ
いては、製造業、非製造業とも悪化が見込まれている。

個人消費（小売売上、自動車販売）
9月の小売販売額（名目）は前年比＋0.7％と小幅ながら3ヵ月連続でプラスとなった。実質値を試算する
と、前年比－1.7％と4ヵ月連続でマイナスとなった。
9月の新車販売は、前年比＋3.3％と6ヵ月連続で増加。軽自動車が減少したが、普通車、小型車が前
年を上回った。

個人消費（業態別）
9月の業態別小売販売（名目）をみると、スーパー（中部5県）は前年比＋1.6％と2ヵ月ぶりに増加した。
9月の百貨店売上（名古屋）は、前年比－0.2％と14ヵ月ぶりに減少した。高額品やインバウンド向け売上
